<p class="mw-empty-elt">
 
</p>

<p><b>John the Baptist</b><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1st century BC</span> – <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;AD 30</span>) was a <a href="/wiki/Judaean" class="mw-redirect" title="Judaean">Judaean</a> mission preacher active in the area of <a href="/wiki/Jordan_River" title="Jordan River">Jordan River</a> in the early 1st century AD.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He is also known as <b>John the Forerunner</b> in <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>, <b>John the Immerser</b> in some <a href="/wiki/Baptists" title="Baptists">Baptist</a> <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christian</a> traditions,<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> and <b><a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist_in_Islam" title="John the Baptist in Islam">Prophet Yahya</a></b> in <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>. He is sometimes alternatively referred to as <b>John the Baptiser</b>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John is mentioned by the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Roman_Empire" title="History of the Jews in the Roman Empire">Roman Jewish</a> historian <a href="/wiki/Josephus" title="Josephus">Josephus</a><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and he is revered as a major religious figure<sup id="cite_ref-ActJJohn_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ActJJohn-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> in Christianity, Islam, the <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Compilations_1983_475_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Compilations_1983_475-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Druze" title="Druze">Druze Faith</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mandaeism" title="Mandaeism">Mandaeism</a>, the latter in which he is considered to be the final and most vital prophet. He is considered to be a <a href="/wiki/Prophet" title="Prophet">prophet</a> of <a href="/wiki/God_in_Abrahamic_religions" title="God in Abrahamic religions">God</a> by all of the aforementioned faiths, and is honoured as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in many <a href="/wiki/Christian_denomination" title="Christian denomination">Christian denominations</a>. According to the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>, John anticipated a <a href="/wiki/Messiah" title="Messiah">messianic figure</a> greater than himself,<sup id="cite_ref-ActJMark_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ActJMark-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospels</a> portray John as the precursor or forerunner of <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-marginal_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-marginal-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Jesus himself identifies John as "<a href="/wiki/Elijah" title="Elijah">Elijah</a> who is to come",<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> which is a direct reference to the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Malachi" title="Book of Malachi">Book of Malachi</a> (Malachi 4:5),<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> that has been confirmed by the angel who announced John's birth to his father, <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(New_Testament_figure)" title="Zechariah (New Testament figure)">Zechariah</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a>, John and Jesus were relatives.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some scholars maintain that John belonged to the <a href="/wiki/Essenes" title="Essenes">Essenes</a>, a semi-<a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">ascetic</a> Jewish sect who expected a messiah and practiced ritual <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> John used baptism as the central symbol or <a href="/wiki/Sacrament" title="Sacrament">sacrament</a><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> of his pre-messianic movement. Most biblical scholars agree that John <a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus" title="Baptism of Jesus">baptized Jesus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> and several New Testament accounts report that some of Jesus' early followers had previously been followers of John.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the New Testament, John was sentenced to death and <a href="/wiki/Beheading_of_John_the_Baptist" title="Beheading of John the Baptist">subsequently beheaded</a> by <a href="/wiki/Herod_Antipas" title="Herod Antipas">Herod Antipas</a> around AD 30 after John rebuked him for divorcing his wife <a href="/wiki/Phasa%27el" title="Phasa&#39;el">Phasaelis</a> and then unlawfully wedding <a href="/wiki/Herodias" title="Herodias">Herodias</a>, the wife of his brother <a href="/wiki/Herod_II" title="Herod II">Herod Philip I</a>. Josephus also mentions John in the <i><a href="/wiki/Antiquities_of_the_Jews" title="Antiquities of the Jews">Antiquities of the Jews</a></i> and states that he was executed by order of Herod Antipas in the fortress at <a href="/wiki/Machaerus" title="Machaerus">Machaerus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:402px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg/400px-Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg" decoding="async" width="400" height="234" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg/600px-Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg/800px-Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6083" data-file-height="3566" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder_-_The_Sermon_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>The Preaching of St. John the Baptist</i> by <a href="/wiki/Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder" title="Pieter Bruegel the Elder">Pieter Bruegel the Elder</a>, 1566</div></div></div>
<p>Followers of John existed well into the 2nd century AD, and some proclaimed him to be the messiah.<sup id="cite_ref-CulpepperAnderson2017_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CulpepperAnderson2017-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> In modern times, the followers of John the Baptist are the <a href="/wiki/Mandaeans" title="Mandaeans">Mandaeans</a>, an ancient <a href="/wiki/Ethnoreligious_group" title="Ethnoreligious group">ethnoreligious group</a> who believe that he is their greatest and final prophet.<sup id="cite_ref-Buckley_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buckley-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Drower_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Drower-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Gospel_narratives">Gospel narratives</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Gospel narratives">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus" title="Baptism of Jesus">Baptism of Jesus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Beheading_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist" class="mw-redirect" title="Beheading of Saint John the Baptist">Beheading of Saint John the Baptist</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg/220px-Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="263" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg/330px-Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg/440px-Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2857" data-file-height="3420" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Onorio_Marinari_Salom%C3%A9_con_la_cabeza_del_Bautista_1680_Minneapolis_MA.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Salome" title="Salome">Salome</a> is given the severed head of John the Baptist, <a href="/wiki/Onorio_Marinari" title="Onorio Marinari">Onorio Marinari</a>, 1670s</div></div></div>
<p>John the Baptist is mentioned in all four canonical <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospels</a> and the non-canonical <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_the_Nazarenes" title="Gospel of the Nazarenes">Gospel of the Nazarenes</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels" title="Synoptic Gospels">Synoptic Gospels</a> (<a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">Mark</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Matthew</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Luke</a>) describe John baptising Jesus; in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a> this is inferred by many to be found in John 1:32.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Strauss2011_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strauss2011-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Mark">In Mark</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: In Mark">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Gospel of Mark introduces John as a fulfillment of a prophecy from the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Isaiah" title="Book of Isaiah">Book of Isaiah</a> (in fact, a conflation of texts from Isaiah, <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Malachi" title="Book of Malachi">Malachi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Exodus" title="Book of Exodus">Exodus</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-Carl_R._Kazmierski_1996_page_31_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carl_R._Kazmierski_1996_page_31-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> about a messenger being sent ahead, and a voice crying out in the wilderness. John is described as wearing clothes of camel's hair, and living on <a href="/wiki/Locust" title="Locust">locusts</a> and wild <a href="/wiki/Honey" title="Honey">honey</a>. John proclaims baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sin, and says another will come after him who will not baptize with water, but with the Holy Spirit.
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:322px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg/320px-Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg" decoding="async" width="320" height="225" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg/480px-Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg/640px-Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1880" data-file-height="1324" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Massimo_Stanzione_-_Beheading_of_St_John_the_Baptist_-_WGA21701.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Beheading of St John the Baptist</i> by <a href="/wiki/Massimo_Stanzione" title="Massimo Stanzione">Massimo Stanzione</a>, 1635</div></div></div>
<p>Jesus comes to John, and is baptized by him in the river Jordan. The account describes how, as he emerges from the water, Jesus sees the heavens open and the Holy Spirit descends on him "like a dove", and he hears a voice from heaven that says, "You are my Son, the Beloved; with you I am well pleased".<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later in the gospel there is an account of John's death. It is introduced by an incident where the Tetrarch <a href="/wiki/Herod_Antipas" title="Herod Antipas">Herod Antipas</a>, hearing stories about Jesus, imagines that this is John the Baptist raised from the dead. It then explains that John had rebuked Herod for marrying <a href="/wiki/Herodias" title="Herodias">Herodias</a>, the ex-wife of his brother (named here as Philip). Herodias demands his execution, but Herod, who "liked to listen" to John, is reluctant to do so because he fears him, knowing he is a "righteous and holy man".
</p><p>The account then describes how Herodias's unnamed daughter dances before Herod, who is pleased and offers her anything she asks for in return. When the girl asks her mother what she should request, she is told to demand the head of John the Baptist. Reluctantly, Herod orders the beheading of John, and his head is delivered to her, at her request, on a plate. John's disciples take the body away and bury it in a tomb.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are a number of difficulties with this passage. The Gospel refers to Antipas as "King"<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> and the ex-husband of Herodias is named as Philip, but he is known to have been called <a href="/wiki/Herod_II" title="Herod II">Herod</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Gillman2003_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gillman2003-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Although the wording clearly implies the girl was the daughter of Herodias, many texts describe her as "Herod's daughter, Herodias". Since these texts are early and significant and the reading is '<a href="/wiki/Lectio_difficilior_potior" title="Lectio difficilior potior">difficult</a>', many scholars see this as the original version, altered in later versions and in Matthew and Luke.<sup id="cite_ref-Gillman2003_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gillman2003-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Josephus says that Herodias had a daughter by the name of Salome.<sup id="cite_ref-Josephus1999_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Josephus1999-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Scholars have speculated about the origins of the story. Since it shows signs of having been composed in Aramaic, which Mark apparently did not speak, he is likely to have got it from a Palestinian source.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> There are a variety of opinions about how much actual historical material it contains, especially given the alleged factual errors.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Many scholars have seen the story of John arrested, executed, and buried in a tomb as a conscious foreshadowing of the fate of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Matthew">In Matthew</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: In Matthew">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg/220px-Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="286" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg/330px-Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg/440px-Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3296" data-file-height="4286" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mattia_Preti_-_San_Giovanni_Battista_Predicazione.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>St. John the Baptist Preaching</i>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1665</span>, by <a href="/wiki/Mattia_Preti" title="Mattia Preti">Mattia Preti</a></div></div></div>
<p>The Gospel of Matthew account begins with the same modified quotation from Isaiah,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> moving the Malachi and Exodus material to later in the text, where it is quoted by Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-Moyise2011_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Moyise2011-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The description of John is possibly taken directly from Mark ("clothing of camel's hair with a leather belt around his waist, and his food was locusts and wild honey"), along with the proclamation that one was coming who would baptize with the Holy Spirit "and fire".<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The book of Matthew next has Jesus coming to John to be baptized, but John objects because he is not worthy because Jesus is the one that brings the baptism in the Spirit.<sup id="cite_ref-Evans2014_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Evans2014-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Unlike Mark, Matthew describes John as critical of Pharisees and Sadducees and as preaching "the kingdom of heaven is at hand" and a "coming judgment".
</p><p>Matthew shortens the account of the beheading of John, and adds two elements: that Herod Antipas wants John dead, and that the death is reported to Jesus by his disciples.<sup id="cite_ref-Wink2006_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wink2006-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Matthew's approach is to shift the focus away from Herod and onto John as a prototype of Jesus. Where Mark has Herod killing John reluctantly and at Herodias' insistence, Matthew describes him as wanting John dead.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Luke_and_Acts">In Luke and Acts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: In Luke and Acts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Nativity_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist" class="mw-redirect" title="Nativity of Saint John the Baptist">Nativity of Saint John the Baptist</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Piero,_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Piero%2C_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg/220px-Piero%2C_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="321" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Piero%2C_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg/330px-Piero%2C_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Piero%2C_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg/440px-Piero%2C_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2893" data-file-height="4226" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Piero,_battesimo_di_cristo_04.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/The_Baptism_of_Christ_(Piero_della_Francesca)" title="The Baptism of Christ (Piero della Francesca)"><i>The Baptism of Jesus Christ</i>, by Piero della Francesca</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1448–50</span></div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a> adds an account of John's infancy, introducing him as the miraculous son of <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zechariah (priest)">Zechariah</a>, an old priest, and his wife <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_(Biblical_person)" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth (Biblical person)">Elizabeth</a>, who was past <a href="/wiki/Menopause" title="Menopause">menopause</a> and therefore unable to have children.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> According to this account, the birth of John was foretold by the angel <a href="/wiki/Gabriel" title="Gabriel">Gabriel</a> to Zechariah while he was performing his functions as a priest in the temple of Jerusalem. Since he is described as a priest of the course of <a href="/wiki/Abijah" title="Abijah">Abijah</a> and Elizabeth as one of the <a href="/wiki/Daughters_of_Aaron" class="mw-redirect" title="Daughters of Aaron">daughters of Aaron</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> this would make John a descendant of <a href="/wiki/Aaron" title="Aaron">Aaron</a> on both his father's and mother's side.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> On the basis of this account, the Catholic as well as the Anglican and Lutheran liturgical calendars placed the feast of the Nativity of John the Baptist on 24 June, six months before Christmas.<sup id="cite_ref-Lives_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lives-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Elizabeth is described as a "relative" of Mary the mother of Jesus, in Luke 1:36.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> There is no mention of a family relationship between John and Jesus in the other Gospels, and <a href="/wiki/Raymond_E._Brown" title="Raymond E. Brown">Raymond E. Brown</a> has described it as "of dubious <a href="/wiki/Historicity" title="Historicity">historicity</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Geza_Vermes" class="mw-redirect" title="Geza Vermes">Géza Vermes</a> has called it "artificial and undoubtedly Luke's creation".<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> The many similarities between the Gospel of Luke story of the birth of John and the Old Testament account of the birth of <a href="/wiki/Samuel_(Biblical_figure)#Family" class="mw-redirect" title="Samuel (Biblical figure)">Samuel</a> suggest that Luke's account of the annunciation and birth of Jesus are modeled on that of Samuel.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-nativity">Post-nativity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Post-nativity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Unique to the Gospel of Luke, John the Baptist explicitly teaches charity, baptizes tax-collectors, and advises soldiers.
</p><p>The text briefly mentions that John is imprisoned and later beheaded by Herod, but the Gospel of Luke lacks the story of a step-daughter dancing for Herod and requesting John's head.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Acts" class="mw-redirect" title="Book of Acts">Book of Acts</a> portrays some disciples of John becoming followers of Jesus,<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> a development not reported by the gospels except for the early case of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Andrew" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Andrew">Andrew</a>, Simon Peter's brother.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_the_Gospel_of_John">In the Gospel of John</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: In the Gospel of John">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The fourth gospel describes John the Baptist as "a man sent from God" who "was not the light", but "came as a witness, to bear witness to the light, so that through him everyone might believe".<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> John confirms that he is not the Christ nor Elijah nor 'the prophet' when asked by Jewish priests and Pharisees; instead, he described himself as the "voice of one crying in the wilderness".<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon literary analysis, it is clear that John is the "testifier and confessor <i>par excellence</i>", particularly when compared to figures like <a href="/wiki/Nicodemus" title="Nicodemus">Nicodemus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg/170px-Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg/255px-Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg/340px-Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2536" data-file-height="3090" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mathis_Gothart_Gr%C3%BCnewald_024.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Matthias_Gr%C3%BCnewald" title="Matthias Grünewald">Matthias Grünewald</a>, detail of the <i><a href="/wiki/Isenheim_Altarpiece" title="Isenheim Altarpiece">Isenheim Altarpiece</a></i></div></div></div>
<p>Jesus's baptism is implied but not depicted. Unlike the other gospels, it is John himself who testifies to seeing "the Spirit come down from heaven like a dove and rest on him". John explicitly announces that Jesus is the one "who baptizes with the Holy Spirit" and John even professes a "belief that he is the Son of God" and "the Lamb of God".
</p><p>The Gospel of John reports that Jesus' disciples were baptizing and that a debate broke out between some of the disciples of John and another Jew about purification.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> In this debate John argued that Jesus "must become greater," while he (John) "must become less."<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Gospel of John then points out that Jesus' disciples were baptizing more people than John.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Later, the Gospel relates that Jesus regarded John as "a burning and shining lamp, and you were willing to rejoice for a while in his light".<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Comparative_analysis">Comparative analysis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Comparative analysis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>All four Gospels start Jesus' ministry in association with the appearance of John the Baptist.<sup id="cite_ref-Strauss2020_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strauss2020-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Simon J. Joseph has argued that the Gospel demotes the historical John by painting him only as a prophetic forerunner to Jesus whereas his ministry actually complemented Jesus'.<sup id="cite_ref-Joseph2012_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joseph2012-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="The_prophecy_of_Isaiah">The prophecy of Isaiah</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: The prophecy of Isaiah">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Although the Gospel of Mark implies that the arrival of John the Baptist is the fulfilment of a prophecy from the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Isaiah" title="Book of Isaiah">Book of Isaiah</a>, the words quoted ("I will send my messenger ahead of you, who will prepare your way – a voice of one calling in the wilderness, 'Prepare the way for the Lord, make straight paths for him.'") are actually a composite of texts from <a href="/wiki/Second_Isaiah" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Isaiah">Isaiah</a>, <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Malachi" title="Book of Malachi">Malachi</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Exodus" title="Book of Exodus">Book of Exodus</a>. (Matthew and Luke drop the first part of the reference.)<sup id="cite_ref-Carl_R._Kazmierski_1996_page_31_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carl_R._Kazmierski_1996_page_31-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Baptism_of_Jesus">Baptism of Jesus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Baptism of Jesus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The gospels differ on the details of the Baptism. In Mark and Luke, Jesus himself sees the heavens open and hears a voice address him personally, saying, "You are my dearly loved son; you bring me great joy". They do not clarify whether others saw and heard these things. Although other incidents where the "voice came out of heaven" are recorded in which, for the sake of the crowds, it was heard audibly, John did say in his witness that he did see the spirit coming down "out of heaven" (John 12:28–30, John 1:32).
</p><p>In Matthew, the voice from heaven does not address Jesus personally, saying instead "This is my beloved son, in whom I am well pleased."
</p><p>In the Gospel of John, John the Baptist himself sees the spirit descend as a dove, testifying about the experience as evidence of Jesus's status.
</p>
<h4><span id="John.27s_knowledge_of_Jesus"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="John's_knowledge_of_Jesus">John's knowledge of Jesus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: John&#039;s knowledge of Jesus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>John's knowledge of Jesus varies across gospels. In the Gospel of Mark, John preaches of a coming leader, but shows no signs of recognizing that Jesus is this leader. In Matthew, however, John immediately recognizes Jesus and John questions his own worthiness to baptize Jesus. In both Matthew and Luke, John later dispatches disciples to question Jesus about his status, asking "Are you he who is to come, or shall we look for another?" In Luke, John is a familial relative of Jesus whose birth was foretold by Gabriel. In the Gospel of John, John the Baptist himself sees the spirit descend like a dove and he explicitly preaches that Jesus is the Son of God.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="John_and_Elijah">John and Elijah</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: John and Elijah">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Matthew_3:4" title="Matthew 3:4">Matthew 3:4</a></div>
<p>The Gospels vary in their depiction of John's relationship to <a href="/wiki/Elijah" title="Elijah">Elijah</a>. Matthew and Mark describe John's attire in a way reminiscent of the description of Elijah in <a href="/wiki/2_Kings_1:8" class="mw-redirect" title="2 Kings 1:8">2 Kings 1:8</a>, who also wore a garment of hair and a leather belt. In Matthew, Jesus explicitly teaches that John is "Elijah who was to come" (Matthew 11:14 – see also Matthew 17:11–13); many Christian theologians have taken this to mean that John was Elijah's successor. In the Gospel of John, John the Baptist explicitly denies being Elijah. In the annunciation narrative in Luke, an angel appears to Zechariah, John's father, and tells him that John "will turn many of the sons of Israel to the Lord their God," and that he will go forth "in the spirit and power of Elijah."<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The following comparison table is primarily based on the <a href="/wiki/New_International_Version" title="New International Version">New International Version</a> (NIV) English translation of the New Testament.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The account of <a href="/wiki/Flavius_Josephus" class="mw-redirect" title="Flavius Josephus">Flavius Josephus</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/Antiquities_of_the_Jews" title="Antiquities of the Jews">Antiquities of the Jews</a></i> was translated by William Whiston.<sup id="cite_ref-Josephus18_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Josephus18-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<table class="wikitable float-left">
<tbody><tr>

<td>John 1:19–42, 3:22–36, 4:1
<ul><li>John the Baptist preached to people and baptised them in the Jordan. He denied being the <a href="/wiki/Messiah" title="Messiah">Messiah</a>.</li>
<li>It is unstated whether or not John the Baptist baptised Jesus. He insisted Jesus was superior: the <a href="/wiki/Son_of_God" title="Son of God">Son</a>/<a href="/wiki/Lamb_of_God" title="Lamb of God">Lamb of God</a>.</li>
<li>Two of John the Baptist's disciples – including <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew</a> – defected to Jesus at John's own insistence.</li>
<li>John the Baptist baptised at Enon/Salim before being arrested. His disciples told him Jesus was successful; John endorsed Jesus as his superior and the Son of God.</li>
<li>Jesus heard the rumour he was more successful than John.</li></ul>
</td>
<td><i>Jewish Antiquities</i> 18. 5. 2.
<ul><li>John the Baptist preached to people and baptised them.</li></ul>

<td><i>Prison</i></td>
<td>Matthew 11:2–7, 14:6–12
<ul><li>John the Baptist criticised king <a href="/wiki/Herod_Antipas" title="Herod Antipas">Herod Antipas</a> for marrying <a href="/wiki/Herod_II" title="Herod II">his brother</a>'s ex-wife <a href="/wiki/Herodias" title="Herodias">Herodias</a>.</li>
<li>John the Baptist was therefore arrested by Herod Antipas.</li>
<li>John the Baptist, in prison, heard about Jesus' deeds, sent some disciples to ask if Jesus was the awaited one. Jesus listed his miracles and said: 'Blessed is he who doesn't reject me'. The disciples returned to John the Baptist.</li>
<li>Herod wanted to kill John, but was afraid of the people.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Beheading_of_John_the_Baptist" title="Beheading of John the Baptist">John the Baptist was executed by beheading</a> by Herod Antipas on the request of <a href="/wiki/Salome" title="Salome">Herodias' daughter</a>. His disciples buried his remains and told Jesus.</li></ul>
</td>
<td>Mark 1:14, 6:17–29
<ul><li>John the Baptist criticised king Herod Antipas for marrying his brother's ex-wife Herodias.</li>
<li>John the Baptist was therefore arrested by Herod Antipas.</li>
<li>Herodias wanted John killed, but Herod Antipas protected John because he knew John was a just and holy man.</li>
<li>John the Baptist was executed by beheading by Herod Antipas on the request of Herodias' daughter. His disciples buried his remains.</li></ul>
</td>
<td>Luke 3:19–20, 7:18–25, 9:9
<ul><li>John the Baptist criticised king Herod Antipas for marrying his brother's ex-wife Herodias and other evils.</li>
<li>John the Baptist was therefore arrested by Herod Antipas.</li>
<li>John the Baptist [in prison?] heard about Jesus' deeds (in <a href="/wiki/Capernaum" title="Capernaum">Capernaum</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nein" title="Nein">Nain</a>), sent 2 disciples to ask if Jesus was the awaited one. Jesus listed his miracles and said: 'Blessed is he who doesn't reject me.' The disciples returned to John the Baptist.</li>
<li>[no execution motive mentioned]</li>
<li>John the Baptist was executed by beheading by Herod Antipas.</li></ul>
</td>
<td>John 3:24
<ul><li>[no arrest motive mentioned]</li>
<li>John the Baptist was arrested.</li>
<li>[no execution motive mentioned]</li>
<li>[no execution mentioned]</li></ul>
</td>
<td><i>Jewish Antiquities</i> 18. 5. 2.
<ul><li>John the Baptist gained a large following.</li>
<li>Herod Antipas feared the widely popular John the Baptist would incite his followers to launch a rebellion against his rule.</li>
<li>Therefore, he had John the Baptist arrested and imprisoned at <a href="/wiki/Machaerus" title="Machaerus">Macherus</a>.</li>
<li>Herod Antipas later had John the Baptist executed 'to prevent any mischief he might cause, and not bring himself into difficulties'.</li></ul>
</td></tr>

</td>
<td>Mark 6:14–16

<li>Herod Antipas agreed with those saying Jesus was actually John the Baptist risen from the dead.</li></ul>

<ul><li>Word of Jesus' miracles spread; some people believed Jesus was actually John the Baptist risen from the dead, others believed he was Elijah, still others that an old prophet had risen.</li>
<li>Herod Antipas didn't believe Jesus was John the Baptist, but had to be someone else.</li></ul>
</td>

<ul><li>Jesus said his claims were reliable, because he knew John the Baptist's testimony about Jesus was reliable, even though Jesus didn't need human testimony.</li></ul>

</p>
