</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below" style="border-top:1px lightblue solid; border-bottom:1px lightblue solid;">
<a href="/wiki/Portal:Religion" title="Portal:Religion">Religion portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Mandaeism" title="Template:Mandaeism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Mandaeism" title="Template talk:Mandaeism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Mandaeism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>John the Baptist, or Yuhana Maṣbana (<a href="/wiki/Classical_Mandaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Mandaic language">Classical Mandaic</a>: <span lang="myz" dir="rtl">ࡉࡅࡄࡀࡍࡀ ࡌࡀࡑࡁࡀࡍࡀ</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Literal_translation" title="Literal translation">lit.</a>&#8201;</small>&#39;John the Baptizer&#39; <span title="Classical Mandaic-language romanization"><i lang="myz-Latn">Iuhana Maṣbana</i></span>)<sup id="cite_ref-GR_Gelbert_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GR_Gelbert-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> is considered the greatest prophet of the <a href="/wiki/Mandaean" class="mw-redirect" title="Mandaean">Mandaeans</a>. Mandaeans also refer to him as <span title="Classical Mandaic-language romanization"><i lang="myz-Latn">Yuhana bar Zakria</i></span> (John, son of Zechariah).<sup id="cite_ref-Gelbert_2017_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gelbert_2017-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> He plays a large part in their religious texts such as the <a href="/wiki/Ginza_Rabba" title="Ginza Rabba">Ginza Rabba</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Mandaean_Book_of_John" title="Mandaean Book of John">Mandaean Book of John</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Mandaeans believe that they descend directly from John's original disciples<sup id="cite_ref-DrowerHaranGawaita_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DrowerHaranGawaita-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> but they do not believe that their religion began with John, tracing their beliefs back to their first prophet Adam.<sup id="cite_ref-Drower_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Drower-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 3">&#58;&#8202;3&#8202;</span></sup> According to Mandaeism, John was a great teacher, a <a href="/wiki/Nazarene_(sect)#Nasoraean_Mandaeans" title="Nazarene (sect)">Nasoraean</a> and renewer of the faith.<sup id="cite_ref-Buckley_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buckley-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 24">&#58;&#8202;24&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> John is a messenger of Light (<span title="Classical Mandaic-language romanization"><i lang="myz-Latn">nhura</i></span>) and Truth (<span title="Classical Mandaic-language romanization"><i lang="myz-Latn"><a href="/wiki/Kushta" title="Kushta">kushta</a></i></span>) who possessed the power of healing and full <a href="/wiki/Gnosis" title="Gnosis">Gnosis</a> (<span title="Classical Mandaic-language romanization"><i lang="myz-Latn"><a href="/wiki/Manda_(Mandaeism)" title="Manda (Mandaeism)">manda</a></i></span>).<sup id="cite_ref-BSN_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BSN-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 48">&#58;&#8202;48&#8202;</span></sup> Mandaean texts make it abundantly clear that early Mandaeans were extremely loyal to John and viewed him as a prophetic reformer of the ancient Mandaean/Israelite tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-BuckleyOrigins_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BuckleyOrigins-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 108">&#58;&#8202;108&#8202;</span></sup> Scholars such as <a href="/wiki/Mark_Lidzbarski" title="Mark Lidzbarski">Mark Lidzbarski</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rudolf_Mac%C3%BAch" title="Rudolf Macúch">Rudolf Macúch</a>, <a href="/wiki/E._S._Drower" title="E. S. Drower">Ethel S. Drower</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jorunn_J._Buckley" class="mw-redirect" title="Jorunn J. Buckley">Jorunn J. Buckley</a>, and <a href="/w/index.php?title=%C5%9Einasi_G%C3%BCnd%C3%BCz&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Şinasi Gündüz (page does not exist)">Şinasi Gündüz</a><sup class="noprint" style="font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://tr.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Einasi_G%C3%BCnd%C3%BCz" class="extiw" title="tr:Şinasi Gündüz">tr</a>&#93;</sup> believe that the Mandaeans likely have a historical connection with John's original disciples.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Buckley_44-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buckley-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Mandaeans believe that John was married, with his wife named Anhar, and had children.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Nasoraia_2021_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nasoraia_2021-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Enišbai (<a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_(biblical_figure)" title="Elizabeth (biblical figure)">Elizabeth</a>) is mentioned as the mother of John the Baptist in chapters 18, 21, and 32 of the <a href="/wiki/Mandaean_Book_of_John" title="Mandaean Book of John">Mandaean Book of John</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ddy_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ddy-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Gelbert_2017_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gelbert_2017-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Islam">Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist_in_Islam" title="John the Baptist in Islam">John the Baptist in Islam</a></div>
<p>John the Baptist is known as Yaḥyā ibn Zakariyā (<a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">يحيى بن زكريا</span>)<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> in Islam and is honoured as a <a href="/wiki/Prophets_and_messengers_in_Islam" title="Prophets and messengers in Islam">prophet</a>. He is believed by <a href="/wiki/Muslim" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim">Muslims</a> to have been a witness to the <a href="/wiki/Revelation" title="Revelation">word of God</a>, and a <a href="/wiki/Prophet" title="Prophet">prophet</a> who would herald the coming of <a href="/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam" title="Jesus in Islam">Jesus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> His father <a href="/wiki/Islamic_view_of_Zechariah" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic view of Zechariah">Zechariah</a> was also an Islamic prophet. <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islamic</a> tradition maintains that John met  <a href="/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam" title="Muhammad in Islam">Muhammad</a> on the night of the <a href="/wiki/Isra_and_Mi%27raj" class="mw-redirect" title="Isra and Mi&#39;raj">Mi'raj</a>, along with Jesus in the second heaven.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> John's story was also told to the <a href="/wiki/Aksumite_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Aksumite Empire">Abyssinian</a> king during the Muslim refugees' <a href="/wiki/Migration_to_Abyssinia" title="Migration to Abyssinia">Migration to Abyssinia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>, John was one on whom God sent peace on the day that he was born and the day that he died.<sup id="cite_ref-Cite_quran&#124;19&#124;13&#124;e=15&#124;s=ns_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cite_quran|19|13|e=15|s=ns-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Quranic_mentions">Quranic mentions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Quranic mentions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a> claims that John the Baptist was the first to receive this name (<a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Perseus%3Atext%3A2002.02.0006%3Asura%3D19%3Averse%3D7">19:7–10</a>) but since the name Yoḥanan occurs many times before John the Baptist,<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> this verse is referring either to Islamic scholar consensus that "Yaḥyā" is not the same name as "Yoḥanan"<sup id="cite_ref-islamic-awareness.org_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-islamic-awareness.org-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> or to the <a href="/wiki/Biblical" class="mw-redirect" title="Biblical">Biblical</a> account of the <a href="/wiki/Miracle" title="Miracle">miraculous</a> naming of John, which accounted that he was almost named "Zacharias" (Greek: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Ζαχαρίας</span></span>)<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> after his father's name, as no one in the <a href="/wiki/Lineage_(anthropology)" title="Lineage (anthropology)">lineage</a> of his father <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zechariah (priest)">Zacharias</a> (also known as <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zechariah (priest)">Zechariah</a>) had been named "John" ("Yohanan"/"Yoannes") before him.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>, <a href="/wiki/God_in_Islam" title="God in Islam">God</a> frequently mentions Zechariah's continuous praying for the birth of a son. Zechariah's wife, mentioned in the New Testament as <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_(Biblical_person)" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth (Biblical person)">Elizabeth</a>, was barren and therefore the birth of a child seemed impossible.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> As a gift from God, Zechariah (or Zakaria) was given a son by the name of "Yaḥya" or "John", a name specially chosen for this child alone. In accordance with Zechariah's prayer, God made John and Jesus, who according to <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a> was born six months later,<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> renew the message of God, which had been corrupted and lost by the <a href="/wiki/Israelites" title="Israelites">Israelites</a>. As the Quran says:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>(His prayer was answered): "O Zakariya! We give thee good news of a son: His name shall be Yahya: on none by that name have We conferred distinction before."
</p><p>He said: "O my Lord! How shall I have a son, when my wife is barren and I have grown quite decrepit from old age?"
</p><p>He said: "So (it will be) thy Lord saith, 'that is easy for Me: I did indeed create thee before, when thou hadst been nothing!'"
</p><p>
(Zakarya) said: "O my Lord! give me a Sign." "Thy Sign," was the answer, "Shall be that thou shalt speak to no man for three nights."</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>Quran, <a href="/wiki/Sura" class="mw-redirect" title="Sura">sura</a> 19 (<a href="/wiki/Maryam_(sura)" class="mw-redirect" title="Maryam (sura)">Maryam</a>), <a href="/wiki/Ayah" class="mw-redirect" title="Ayah">verse</a> 7<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>John was exhorted to hold fast to the <a href="/wiki/Scripture" class="mw-redirect" title="Scripture">Scripture</a> and was given wisdom by God while still a child.<sup id="cite_ref-Cite_quran&#124;19&#124;12&#124;s=ns_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cite_quran|19|12|s=ns-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> He was pure and devout, and walked well in the presence of God. He was dutiful towards his parents and he was not arrogant or rebellious. John's reading and understanding of the scriptures, when only a child, surpassed even that of the greatest scholars of the time.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_179-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Muslim" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim">Muslim</a> <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a> narrates that <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> sent John out with twelve disciples,<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> who preached the message before Jesus called his own disciples.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_180-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> The Quran says:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>"O Yaḥya! take hold of the Book with might": and We gave him Wisdom even as a youth, </p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>Quran, sura 19 (<a href="/wiki/Maryam_(sura)" class="mw-redirect" title="Maryam (sura)">Maryam</a>), ayah 12<sup id="cite_ref-Cite_quran&#124;19&#124;12&#124;s=ns_182-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cite_quran|19|12|s=ns-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>John was a classical prophet,<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> who was exalted high by God for his bold denouncing of all things sinful. Furthermore, the Quran speaks of John's gentle piety and love and his humble attitude towards life, for which he was granted the Purity of Life:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And piety as from Us, and purity: He was devout,</p><div class="paragraphbreak" style="margin-top:0.5em"></div><p>And kind to his parents, and he was not overbearing or rebellious.</p><div class="paragraphbreak" style="margin-top:0.5em"></div><p>So Peace on him the day he was born, the day that he dies, and the day that he will be raised up to life (again)!</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>Quran, sura 19 (<a href="/wiki/Maryam_(sura)" class="mw-redirect" title="Maryam (sura)">Maryam</a>), ayah 13–15<sup id="cite_ref-Cite_quran&#124;19&#124;13&#124;e=15&#124;s=ns_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cite_quran|19|13|e=15|s=ns-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>John is also honored highly in <a href="/wiki/Sufism" title="Sufism">Sufism</a> as well as <a href="/wiki/Islamic" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic">Islamic</a> <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mysticism</a>, primarily because of the Quran's description of John's chastity and kindness.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Sufi" class="mw-redirect" title="Sufi">Sufis</a> have frequently applied commentaries on the passages on John in the Quran, primarily concerning the God-given gift of "Wisdom" which he acquired in youth as well as his parallels with <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a>. Although several phrases used to describe John and Jesus are virtually identical in the Quran, the manner in which they are expressed is different.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Druze_view">Druze view</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Druze view">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Maqam_Yahya_(John_the_Baptist).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Maqam_Yahya_%28John_the_Baptist%29.jpg/220px-Maqam_Yahya_%28John_the_Baptist%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Maqam_Yahya_%28John_the_Baptist%29.jpg/330px-Maqam_Yahya_%28John_the_Baptist%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Maqam_Yahya_%28John_the_Baptist%29.jpg/440px-Maqam_Yahya_%28John_the_Baptist%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="720" data-file-height="540" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Maqam_Yahya_(John_the_Baptist).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Druze Maqam al-Nabi Yahya (John the Baptist) in <a href="/wiki/As-Suwayda_Governorate" title="As-Suwayda Governorate">As-Suwayda Governorate</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Druze tradition honors several "mentors" and "prophets", and John the Baptist is honored as a <a href="/wiki/Prophet" title="Prophet">prophet</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Druze" title="Druze">Druze</a> venerate John the Baptist and he is considered a central figure in <a href="/wiki/Druzism" class="mw-redirect" title="Druzism">Druzism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Druze, like some <a href="/wiki/Christians" title="Christians">Christians</a>, believe that <a href="/wiki/Elijah" title="Elijah">Elijah</a> (al-<a href="/wiki/Khidr" title="Khidr">Khidr</a>) came back as John the Baptist,<sup id="cite_ref-auto_188-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> since they believe in <a href="/wiki/Reincarnation" title="Reincarnation">reincarnation</a> and the transmigration of the soul.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Bah.C3.A1.CA.BC.C3.AD_view"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Baháʼí_view">Baháʼí view</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Baháʼí view">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a> considers John to have been a prophet of God who like all other prophets was sent to instill the knowledge of God, promote unity among the people of the world, and to show people the correct way to live.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> There are numerous quotations in the writings of <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%27u%27ll%C3%A1h" class="mw-redirect" title="Bahá&#39;u&#39;lláh">Bahá'u'lláh</a>, founder of the <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a>, mentioning John the Baptist. He is regarded by Baháʼís as a <a href="/wiki/Manifestation_of_God_(Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith)#Minor_prophets" title="Manifestation of God (Baháʼí Faith)">lesser Prophet</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Compilations_1983_475_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Compilations_1983_475-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Bahá'u'lláh claimed that his forerunner, the <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A1b" title="Báb">Báb</a>, was the spiritual return of John the Baptist. In his letter to Pope <a href="/wiki/Pius_IX" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius IX">Pius IX</a>, Bahá'u'lláh wrote:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>O followers of the Son! We have once again sent John unto you, and He, verily, hath cried out in the wilderness of the <a href="/wiki/Persian_Bay%C3%A1n" title="Persian Bayán">Bayán</a>: O peoples of the world! Cleanse your eyes! The Day whereon ye can behold the Promised One and attain unto Him hath drawn nigh! O followers of the Gospel! Prepare the way! The Day of the advent of the Glorious Lord is at hand! Make ready to enter the Kingdom. Thus hath it been ordained by God, He Who causeth the dawn to break.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>John is believed to have had the specific role of foretelling and preparing the way for Jesus. In condemning those who had 'turned aside' from him, Bahá'u'lláh compared them to the followers of John the Baptist, who, he said, "protested against Him Who was the Spirit (Jesus) saying: 'The dispensation of John hath not yet ended; wherefore hast thou come?'" Bahá'u'lláh believed that the Báb played the same role as John in preparing the people for his own coming. As such, Bahá'u'lláh refers to the Báb as 'My Forerunner', the Forerunner being a title that Christians reserve for John the Baptist.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
However, Baháʼís consider the <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A1b" title="Báb">Báb</a> to be a greater Prophet (<a href="/wiki/Manifestation_of_God_(Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith)" title="Manifestation of God (Baháʼí Faith)">Manifestation of God</a>) and thus possessed of a far greater station than John the Baptist.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Scholarship">Scholarship</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Scholarship">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Giovanni_di_paolo,_scene_della_vita_del_battista,_1454,_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Giovanni_di_paolo%2C_scene_della_vita_del_battista%2C_1454%2C_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg/220px-Giovanni_di_paolo%2C_scene_della_vita_del_battista%2C_1454%2C_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="154" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Giovanni_di_paolo%2C_scene_della_vita_del_battista%2C_1454%2C_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg/330px-Giovanni_di_paolo%2C_scene_della_vita_del_battista%2C_1454%2C_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Giovanni_di_paolo%2C_scene_della_vita_del_battista%2C_1454%2C_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg/440px-Giovanni_di_paolo%2C_scene_della_vita_del_battista%2C_1454%2C_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4692" data-file-height="3288" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Giovanni_di_paolo,_scene_della_vita_del_battista,_1454,_02_partenza_per_il_deserto_1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>John setting off into the desert, <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_di_Paolo" title="Giovanni di Paolo">Giovanni di Paolo</a>, 1454</div></div></div>
<p>Scholars studying John the Baptist's relationship with Jesus of Nazareth have commented on the differences in their respective approaches.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/James_F._McGrath" title="James F. McGrath">James F. McGrath</a> writes "In the first half of the 20th century, the <a href="/wiki/Mandaeans" title="Mandaeans">Mandaeans</a> received significant attention from New Testament scholars who thought that their high view of John the Baptist might mean they were the descendants of his disciples. Many historians think that Jesus of Nazareth was a disciple of John the Baptist before breaking away to form his own movement, and I am inclined to agree."<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/L._Michael_White" title="L. Michael White">L. Michael White</a> says John the Baptist should be thought of "...primarily as one who was calling for a return to an intensely Jewish piety [...] to follow the way of the Lord [...] to make oneself pure... to be right with God [...] And it seems to be that he calls for baptism as a sign of rededication or repurification of life in a typically Jewish way before God."<sup id="cite_ref-pbs_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pbs-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/John_Dominic_Crossan" title="John Dominic Crossan">John Dominic Crossan</a> sees John the Baptist as an apocalyptic eschatologist, whose message was that "God, very soon, imminently, any moment, is going to descend to eradicate the evil of this world in a sort of an apocalyptic consummation..."<sup id="cite_ref-pbs_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pbs-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> When Jesus says John is the greatest person ever born on earth, but the least in the Kingdom of God is greater than John, it means Jesus is changing his vision of God and the Kingdom of God from what he has taken from John. For Crossan, Jesus is an ethical eschatologist that sees "...the demand that God is making on us, not us on God so much as God on us, to do something about the evil in the world."<sup id="cite_ref-pbs_195-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pbs-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Michael H. Crosby, O.F.M.Cap. states there was "no biblical evidence indicating that John the Baptist ever became a disciple of Jesus." He believes that John's concept of what a messiah should be was in contrast to how Jesus presented himself, and kept him from becoming a disciple of Jesus. Crosby states, "an unbiased  reading leaves us with the figure of John the Baptist as a reformist Jew who also may have wanted desperately to become a believer but was unable to become convinced of Jesus' messiahship..."<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> Crosby considers John's effectiveness as a "precursor" in encouraging others to follow Jesus as very minimal, since the scriptures record only two of his own followers having become Jesus' disciples.
</p><p>Professor <a href="/wiki/Candida_Moss" title="Candida Moss">Candida Moss</a> noted that John and Jesus become "de facto competitors in the ancient religious marketplace." Even after baptizing Jesus, John did not follow Jesus but maintained a separate ministry. After John's death, Jesus' followers had to differentiate him from the executed prophet, "countering the prevalent idea that Jesus was actually John raised from the dead." Moss also references the incident in Matthew 16 where disciples indicated some people believed Jesus was John the Baptist.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pastor Robert L. Deffinbaugh views John's sending two of his disciples to ask Jesus if he were the Messiah or whether another should be sought as the Baptist's issuing a public challenge since the message was presented to Jesus while he was with a gathered crowd. Deffinbaugh suggests that John might have been looking for inauguration of the kingdom of God in a more dramatic way than what Jesus was presenting, as John had previously warned that the "Messiah would come with fire." Jesus answered by indicating his miracle works and teachings which themselves gave evidence of his identity: "The blind receive sight, the lame walk, those who have leprosy are cured, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, and the good news is preached to the poor".<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Harold_W._Attridge" title="Harold W. Attridge">Harold W. Attridge</a> agrees with Crossan that John was an apocalyptic preacher. Attridge says most contemporary scholars would see the idea of John as the "forerunner" of Jesus as a construct developed by the early church to help explain the relationship between the two. "For the early church it would have been something of an embarrassment to say that Jesus, who was in their minds superior to John the Baptist, had been baptized by him, and thereby proclaimed some sort of subordination to him, some sort of disciple relationship to him..."<sup id="cite_ref-pbs_195-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pbs-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Barbara_Thiering" title="Barbara Thiering">Barbara Thiering</a> questions the dating of the <a href="/wiki/Dead_Sea_Scrolls" title="Dead Sea Scrolls">Dead Sea Scrolls</a> and suggests that the <a href="/wiki/Teacher_of_Righteousness" title="Teacher of Righteousness">Teacher of Righteousness</a> (leader of the <a href="/wiki/Essenes" title="Essenes">Essenes</a>) preached coming fiery judgment, said "the axe is laid to the roots of the tree", called people "vipers", practiced baptism and lived in the wilderness of Judea. Due to these reasons, she believes there is a strong possibility that the Teacher of Righteousness was John the Baptist.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_art">In art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: In art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_(1620s,_GTG)_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_%281620s%2C_GTG%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_%281620s%2C_GTG%29_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="254" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_%281620s%2C_GTG%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_%281620s%2C_GTG%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_%281620s%2C_GTG%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_%281620s%2C_GTG%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="979" data-file-height="1131" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:John_the_Baptist_by_Prokopiy_Chirin_(1620s,_GTG)_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Eastern Orthodox <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icon</a> <i>John the Baptist – the Angel of the Desert</i> (<a href="/wiki/Stroganov_School" title="Stroganov School">Stroganov School</a>, 1620s) <a href="/wiki/Tretyakov_Gallery" title="Tretyakov Gallery">Tretyakov Gallery</a>, <a href="/wiki/Moscow" title="Moscow">Moscow</a></div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_Christian_art">Early Christian art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Early Christian art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The earliest depictions of St John are found in the <i><a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptism of Christ">Baptism of Christ</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> one of the earliest scenes from the <i><a href="/wiki/Life_of_Christ_in_art" title="Life of Christ in art">Life of Christ</a></i> to be frequently depicted in <a href="/wiki/Early_Christian_art" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Christian art">Early Christian art</a>, and John's tall, thin, even gaunt, and bearded figure is already established by the 5th century. Only he and Jesus are consistently shown with long hair from Early Christian times, when the apostles generally have trim classical cuts; in fact John is more consistently depicted in this way than Jesus.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Byzantine_and_Eastern_Orthodox_art">Byzantine and Eastern Orthodox art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=41" title="Edit section: Byzantine and Eastern Orthodox art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In Byzantine and later Eastern Orthodox art, John the Baptist and the Holy Virgin Mary often flank Jesus on either side. The composition of the <i><a href="/wiki/Deesis" title="Deesis">Deesis</a></i> came to be included in every <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox">Eastern Orthodox</a> church, as remains the case to this day. Here John and the <a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a> (Mary the "God-bearer") flank a <a href="/wiki/Christ_Pantocrator" title="Christ Pantocrator">Christ Pantocrator</a> and intercede for humanity.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Orthodox_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Orthodox Christianity">Orthodox</a> <a href="/wiki/Religious_icon" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious icon">icons</a>, he often has angel's wings, since Mark 1:2<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> describes him as a messenger.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Western_art">Western art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: Western art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After the earliest images showing the Baptism of the Lord follow ones with St John shown as an ascetic wearing camel hair, with a staff and scroll inscribed (in Western art) <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">"Ecce Agnus Dei"</i></span>, or bearing a book or dish with a lamb on it.<sup id="cite_ref-ODCC_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODCC-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Baptist is very often shown on altarpieces designed for churches dedicated to him, where the donor was named for him or where there was some other patronage connection. John the Baptist is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of <a href="/wiki/Florence" title="Florence">Florence</a> and has often been depicted in the art of that city,<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> and also frequently appears in <a href="/wiki/Baptistries" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptistries">baptistries</a>, which are very often dedicated to him.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-204">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Major works depicting St. John the Baptist can be found in the <a href="/wiki/Florence_Baptistery" title="Florence Baptistery">Florence Baptistery</a>, including the mosaics on the vault, the bronze doors by <a href="/wiki/Andrea_Pisano" title="Andrea Pisano">Andrea Pisano</a>, and the great silver altar<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> now in the <a href="/wiki/Museo_dell%27Opera_del_Duomo_(Florence)" title="Museo dell&#39;Opera del Duomo (Florence)">Museo dell'Opera del Duomo</a>.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG/220px-InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="174" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG/330px-InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG/440px-InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="1518" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:InfantJesus_JohnBaptist.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>John the Baptist (right) with the <a href="/wiki/Christ_Child" title="Christ Child">Christ Child</a>, in <i>The Holy Children with a Shell</i> by <a href="/wiki/Bartolom%C3%A9_Esteban_Murillo" title="Bartolomé Esteban Murillo">Bartolomé Esteban Murillo</a></div></div></div>
<p>A number of narrative scenes from his life were often shown on the <a href="/wiki/Predella" title="Predella">predella</a> of altarpieces dedicated to John, and other settings, notably in the frescoes by Giotto for the <a href="https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category:Peruzzi_Chapel" class="extiw" title="c:Category:Peruzzi Chapel">Peruzzi Chapel</a><sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> in the church of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Croce,_Florence" title="Santa Croce, Florence">Santa Croce</a>, the large series in <a href="/wiki/Grisaille" title="Grisaille">grisaille</a> <a href="/wiki/Fresco" title="Fresco">fresco</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Chiostro_dello_Scalzo" title="Chiostro dello Scalzo">Chiostro dello Scalzo</a>, which was <a href="/wiki/Andrea_del_Sarto" title="Andrea del Sarto">Andrea del Sarto</a>'s largest work, and the frescoed <i>Life</i> by <a href="/wiki/Domenico_Ghirlandaio" title="Domenico Ghirlandaio">Domenico Ghirlandaio</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Tornabuoni_Chapel" title="Tornabuoni Chapel">Tornabuoni Chapel</a>, all in Florence. There is another important fresco cycle by <a href="/wiki/Filippo_Lippi" title="Filippo Lippi">Filippo Lippi</a> in <a href="/wiki/Prato_Cathedral" title="Prato Cathedral">Prato Cathedral</a>. These include the typical scenes:<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> the Annunciation to <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zechariah (priest)">Zechariah</a>; John's birth; his naming by his father; the <a href="/wiki/Visitation_(Christianity)" title="Visitation (Christianity)">Visitation</a>; John's departure for the desert; his preaching in the desert; the <a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptism of Christ">Baptism of Christ</a>; John before Herod; the dance of Herod's stepdaughter, <a href="/wiki/Salome" title="Salome">Salome</a>; <a href="/wiki/Beheading_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist" class="mw-redirect" title="Beheading of Saint John the Baptist">his beheading</a>; and the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Herodias" title="Herodias">Herodias</a> Salome carrying his head on a platter.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His birth, which unlike the <a href="/wiki/Nativity_of_Jesus_in_art" title="Nativity of Jesus in art">Nativity of Jesus</a> allowed a relatively wealthy domestic interior to be shown, became increasingly popular as a subject in the late <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-:1_204-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-204">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> with depictions by <a href="/wiki/Jan_van_Eyck" title="Jan van Eyck">Jan van Eyck</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Turin-Milan_Hours" class="mw-redirect" title="Turin-Milan Hours">Turin-Milan Hours</a> and Ghirlandaio in the Tornabuoni Chapel being among the best known. His execution, a church feast-day, was often shown, and by the 15th century scenes such as the dance of Salome became popular; sometimes, as in an <a href="/wiki/Engraving" title="Engraving">engraving</a> by <a href="/wiki/Israhel_van_Meckenem" title="Israhel van Meckenem">Israhel van Meckenem</a>, the interest of the artist is clearly in showing the life of Herod's court, given contemporary dress, as much as the martyrdom of the saint.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> The execution was usually by a swordsman, with John kneeling in prayer, Salome often standing by with an empty platter, and Herod and Herodias at table in a cut-through view of a building in the background.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate,_Southern_Netherlands,_c._1430,_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_(cropped).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate%2C_Southern_Netherlands%2C_c._1430%2C_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_%28cropped%29.JPG/220px-Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate%2C_Southern_Netherlands%2C_c._1430%2C_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_%28cropped%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="204" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate%2C_Southern_Netherlands%2C_c._1430%2C_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_%28cropped%29.JPG/330px-Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate%2C_Southern_Netherlands%2C_c._1430%2C_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_%28cropped%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate%2C_Southern_Netherlands%2C_c._1430%2C_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_%28cropped%29.JPG/440px-Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate%2C_Southern_Netherlands%2C_c._1430%2C_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_%28cropped%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3648" data-file-height="3386" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Head_of_St._John_the_Baptist_on_a_Plate,_Southern_Netherlands,_c._1430,_oak_-_Bode-Museum_-_DSC03181_(cropped).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Head of St. John the Baptist on a Plate, Southern Netherlands, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1430</span>, oak</div></div></div>
<p>Salome bearing John's head on a platter equally became a subject for the <a href="/wiki/Power_of_Women" title="Power of Women">Power of Women</a> group: a Northern Renaissance fashion for images of glamorous but dangerous women (<a href="/wiki/Delilah" title="Delilah">Delilah</a>, <a href="/wiki/Judith" class="mw-redirect" title="Judith">Judith</a> and others).<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> It was often painted by <a href="/wiki/Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder" title="Lucas Cranach the Elder">Lucas Cranach the Elder</a> and engraved by the <a href="/wiki/Little_Masters" title="Little Masters">Little Masters</a>. When the head is brought to the table by Salome, Herod may be shown as startled, if not disgusted, but Herodias is usually not. These images remained popular into the Baroque, with <a href="/wiki/Carlo_Dolci" title="Carlo Dolci">Carlo Dolci</a> painting at least three versions. John preaching, in a landscape setting, was a popular subject in Dutch art from <a href="/wiki/Pieter_Brueghel_the_Elder" class="mw-redirect" title="Pieter Brueghel the Elder">Pieter Brueghel the Elder</a> and his successors.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> The isolated motif of the severed head, often on its platter, was a frequent image, often in sculpture, from the late Middle Ages onwards,<sup id="cite_ref-:2_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> known as <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Ioannes in disco</i></span> (Latin for "John on a plate").
</p><p>As a child (of varying age), he is sometimes shown from the 15th century in family scenes from the life of Christ such as the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Family#In_art" title="Holy Family">Holy Family</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Presentation_of_Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Presentation of Christ">Presentation of Christ</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Marriage_of_the_Virgin" title="Marriage of the Virgin">Marriage of the Virgin</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Kinship" title="Holy Kinship">Holy Kinship</a>. In the Baptism of Christ his presence was obligatory.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>'s two versions of the <i><a href="/wiki/Virgin_of_the_Rocks" title="Virgin of the Rocks">Virgin of the Rocks</a></i> were influential in establishing a Renaissance fashion for variations on the <a href="/wiki/Madonna_and_Child" class="mw-redirect" title="Madonna and Child">Madonna and Child</a> which included John. <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a> in particular painted many compositions of the subject, such as the <i><a href="/wiki/Alba_Madonna" title="Alba Madonna">Alba Madonna</a></i>, <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr"><a href="/wiki/La_belle_jardini%C3%A8re" title="La belle jardinière">La belle jardinière</a></i></span>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Garvagh_Madonna" title="Garvagh Madonna">Garvagh Madonna</a></i>, the <span title="Italian-language text"><i lang="it"><a href="/wiki/Madonna_della_seggiola" class="mw-redirect" title="Madonna della seggiola">Madonna della seggiola</a></i></span>, and the <span title="Italian-language text"><i lang="it"><a href="/wiki/Madonna_dell%27Impannata_(Raphael)" class="mw-redirect" title="Madonna dell&#39;Impannata (Raphael)">Madonna dell'Impannata</a></i></span>, which are among his best-known works.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_(%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27)_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_%28%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_%28%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="134" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_%28%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_%28%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_%28%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_%28%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27%29_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4500" data-file-height="2731" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Everett_Millais_-_Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents_(%60The_Carpenter%27s_Shop%27)_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>St John (right) in <i><a href="/wiki/Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents" title="Christ in the House of His Parents">Christ in the House of His Parents</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/John_Everett_Millais" title="John Everett Millais">John Everett Millais</a>, 1849–50</div></div></div>
<p>John was also often shown by himself as an adolescent or adult, usually already wearing his distinctive dress and carrying a long thin wooden cross<sup id="cite_ref-:0_202-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> – another theme <a href="/wiki/Saint_John_the_Baptist_(Leonardo)" title="Saint John the Baptist (Leonardo)">influenced by Leonardo</a>, whose equivocal composition, with the camel-skin dress, was developed by Raphael, <a href="/wiki/Titian" title="Titian">Titian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Guido_Reni" title="Guido Reni">Guido Reni</a> among many others. Often he is accompanied by a lamb, especially in the many <a href="/wiki/Early_Netherlandish_painting" title="Early Netherlandish painting">Early Netherlandish paintings</a> which needed this attribute as he wore normal clothes, or a red robe over a not very clearly indicated camel skin.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_202-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Caravaggio" title="Caravaggio">Caravaggio</a> painted an especially large number of works depicting John, from <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist_(Caravaggio)" title="John the Baptist (Caravaggio)">at least five largely nude youths</a> attributed to him, to three late works on his death – the great <i><a href="/wiki/The_Beheading_of_Saint_John_the_Baptist_(Caravaggio)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Beheading of Saint John the Baptist (Caravaggio)">Execution</a></i> in Malta, and two sombre Salomes with his head, <a href="/wiki/Salome_with_the_Head_of_John_the_Baptist_(Madrid)_(Caravaggio)" class="mw-redirect" title="Salome with the Head of John the Baptist (Madrid) (Caravaggio)">one in Madrid</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Salome_with_the_Head_of_John_the_Baptist_(Caravaggio,_London)" title="Salome with the Head of John the Baptist (Caravaggio, London)">one in London</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Amiens_Cathedral" title="Amiens Cathedral">Amiens Cathedral</a>, which holds one of the alleged heads of the Baptist, has a biographical sequence in polychrome relief, dating from the 16th century. This includes the execution and the disposal of the saint's remains, which according to legend were burnt in the reign of <a href="/wiki/Julian_the_Apostate" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian the Apostate">Julian the Apostate</a> (4th century) to prevent pilgrimages.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_213-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A remarkable <a href="/wiki/Pre-Raphaelite_Brotherhood" title="Pre-Raphaelite Brotherhood">Pre-Raphaelite</a> portrayal is <i><a href="/wiki/Christ_in_the_House_of_His_Parents" title="Christ in the House of His Parents">Christ in the House of His Parents</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/John_Everett_Millais" title="John Everett Millais">John Everett Millais</a>. Here the Baptist is shown as a child, wearing a loin covering of animal skins, hurrying into <a href="/wiki/Saint_Joseph" title="Saint Joseph">Joseph</a>'s carpenter shop with a bowl of water to join <a href="/wiki/Mary,_mother_of_Jesus" title="Mary, mother of Jesus">Mary</a>, Joseph, and Mary's mother <a href="/wiki/Saint_Anne" title="Saint Anne">Anne</a> in soothing the injured hand of Jesus. Artistic interest enjoyed a considerable revival at the end of the 19th century with <a href="/wiki/Symbolist" class="mw-redirect" title="Symbolist">Symbolist</a> painters such as <a href="/wiki/Gustave_Moreau" title="Gustave Moreau">Gustave Moreau</a> and <a href="/wiki/Puvis_de_Chavannes" class="mw-redirect" title="Puvis de Chavannes">Puvis de Chavannes</a> (<a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a>, London). <a href="/wiki/Oscar_Wilde" title="Oscar Wilde">Oscar Wilde</a>'s play <i><a href="/wiki/Salome_(play)" title="Salome (play)">Salome</a></i> was illustrated by <a href="/wiki/Aubrey_Beardsley" title="Aubrey Beardsley">Aubrey Beardsley</a>, giving rise to some of his most memorable images.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_poetry">In poetry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=43" title="Edit section: In poetry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Italian_Renaissance" title="Italian Renaissance">Italian Renaissance</a> poet <a href="/wiki/Lucrezia_Tornabuoni" title="Lucrezia Tornabuoni">Lucrezia Tornabuoni</a> chose John the Baptist as one of the biblical figures on which she wrote poetry.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He is also referenced in "<a href="/wiki/The_Love_Song_of_J._Alfred_Prufrock" title="The Love Song of J. Alfred Prufrock">The Love Song of J. Alfred Prufrock</a>" by <a href="/wiki/T._S._Eliot" title="T. S. Eliot">T.S. Eliot</a> in <a href="/wiki/Stanza" title="Stanza">stanza</a> <a href="/wiki/12_(number)" title="12 (number)">12</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_music">In music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Baptist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=44" title="Edit section: In music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+link+.ambox{margin-top:-1px}html body.mediawiki .mw-parser-output .ambox.mbox-small-left{margin:4px 1em 4px .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-More_citations_needed plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x,
